## **9. Magnetohydrodynamics and the Blandford-Znajek Mechanism**

To understand the accretion disk and jets, we must consider the interaction between the magnetic field and matter.

### **9.1 The Magnetic Field of the Accretion Disk**

The evolution of the magnetic field inside the accretion disk follows the magnetohydrodynamics (MHD) equations:

`∂B/∂t = ∇×(v×B) - ∇×(η∇×B)`

An important dimensionless number here is the magnetic Reynolds number:

`R_m = vL/η >> 1` (this condition is generally satisfied in accretion disks)

**Magnetic Buoyancy Instability (Parker Instability)** Magnetic field lines rise to the surface of the disk through buoyancy, which is important for corona formation and particle acceleration.

**Magnetorotational Instability (MRI, Balbus-Hawley 1991)** Growth rate: `Γ ≈ Ω` (angular velocity of a prograde Keplerian orbit) MRI is now accepted as the primary mechanism for viscosity and angular momentum transport in accretion disks. Discovered in 1991, this instability represented a major turning point in accretion disk research.

### **9.2 The Blandford-Znajek Mechanism (1977)**

This is the famous theory explaining how powerful jets can be produced from a rotating black hole.

The extracted power output is approximately:

`P_BZ ≈ (κ/4π c) Φ²_BH Ω²_H f(Ω_H)`

where:

- Φ_BH = magnetic flux threading the black hole
    
- Ω_H = a/(2Mr₊) (angular velocity of the event horizon)
    
- κ ≈ 0.044 (numerical coefficient)
    
- f(Ω_H) = efficiency function
    

In the extreme spin limit (a* → 1): `P_BZ ≈ (κ/4π c) Φ²_BH (c/4M)²`

According to this theory, the maximum extractable rotational energy of the black hole is: `E_rot = M - M_irr ≤ 0.29 Mc²`

The appearance of jet-like structures in the simulation when BH_SPIN > 0.6 makes me think this may be related to the Blandford-Znajek mechanism.

### **9.3 Jet Formation**

The Lorentz factor of a relativistic jet is:

`Γ_jet ~ (P_BZ / Ṁ_jet c²)^(1/2)`

Observed values:

- GRB jets: Γ ~ 10² - 10³
    
- AGN jets: Γ ~ 10 - 30
    

Inside the jet, particle acceleration by shocks (Fermi acceleration) occurs:

`dN/dE ∝ E^(-s)`, `s = (r+2)/(r-1)`

where r is the shock compression ratio. For a non-relativistic shock (r=4), s=2, which is similar to the observed cosmic ray spectrum (E^(-2.7)).

---

## **10. Black Hole Thermodynamics and Hawking Radiation**

Although not implemented in this simulation, black hole thermodynamics is a topic that cannot be omitted when discussing black holes.

### **10.1 The Laws of Black Hole Thermodynamics**

- Zeroth Law: The surface gravity κ is constant over the event horizon of a stationary black hole
    
- First Law: `dM = (κ/8π)dA + Ω_H dJ + Φ_H dQ` (energy conservation: change in mass equals contributions from changes in area, angular momentum, and charge)
    
- Second Law: `dA ≥ 0` (the area of the event horizon can never decrease, classically)
    
- Third Law: It is impossible to achieve κ = 0 (the extremal black hole state)
    

### **10.2 Hawking Radiation (Hawking 1974)**

When quantum effects are considered, black holes are not completely black — they emit thermal (blackbody) radiation:

`T_H = ℏκ / (2πck_B) = ℏc³ / (8πGMk_B) ≈ 6.17×10⁻⁸ (M☉/M) K`

The radiation power is: `P = ℏc⁶ / (15360π G²M²) ≈ 3.56×10³² (M☉/M)² W`

The evaporation time is: `t_evap = 5120π G²M³ / (ℏc⁴) ≈ 2.1×10⁶⁷ (M/M☉)³ yr`

The time it takes for a solar-mass black hole to evaporate is 10⁵⁷ times the age of the universe. In other words, Hawking radiation is completely irrelevant for astronomically sized black holes.

### **10.3 Black Hole Entropy (Bekenstein-Hawking)**

Before the discovery of Hawking radiation, Jacob Bekenstein proposed the idea that black holes carry entropy:

`S_BH = k_B A / (4 l_Pl²) = k_B c³ A / (4 Gℏ)`

where `l_Pl = √(Gℏ/c³) ≈ 1.616×10⁻³⁵ m` is the Planck length.

The area of a Kerr black hole is: `A = 4π(r₊² + a²) = 8πM r₊`

The Generalized Second Law states: `dS_BH/dt + dS_matter/dt ≥ 0`

That is, the total entropy — the sum of the black hole entropy and the entropy of surrounding matter — never decreases. If this law holds, the Second Law of Thermodynamics extends to situations involving black holes.

---

## **11. Standard Model Particles and Decay Chains**

Another important feature of this simulation is the implementation of 39 particle species based on PDG 2022 data.

### **11.1 Particle List in the Simulation**

**Leptons:**

- e⁻/e⁺ (0.511 MeV), μ⁻/μ⁺ (105.66 MeV), τ⁻/τ⁺ (1776.86 MeV)
    
- νe, νμ, ντ and their antiparticles (mass approximately 0)
    

**Mesons:**

- π⁺/π⁻ (139.57 MeV, lifetime 2.6×10⁻⁸ s)
    
- π⁰ (134.98 MeV, lifetime 8.5×10⁻¹⁷ s)
    
- K⁺/K⁻ (493.68 MeV), K⁰S/K⁰L
    
- ρ, ω, φ, η
    
- D⁰, D⁺/D⁻, B⁺/B⁻, B⁰
    

**Baryons:**

- p (938.27 MeV, stable)
    
- n (939.57 MeV, lifetime 879 s)
    
- Λ⁰ (1115.68 MeV), Ξ⁻, Ω⁻
    

**Gauge bosons and Higgs:**

- γ (photon, mass 0)
    
- W⁺/W⁻ (80,377 MeV)
    
- Z⁰ (91,188 MeV)
    
- H⁰ (125,200 MeV, Higgs boson)
    

### **11.2 The Strong Coupling Constant (Running Coupling, αs)**

The 2-loop running of the strong coupling constant implemented in this simulation is:

`αs(μ) = αs(MZ) / [1 + (αs(MZ)/2π) b₀ ln(μ/MZ)]`

where `b₀ = (33 - 2nf) / (12π)`, and nf is the number of active quark flavors.

Using the PDG 2022 value (αs(MZ) = 0.1180):

- αs(1 GeV) ≈ 0.5 (strong coupling)
    
- αs(1000 GeV) ≈ 0.08 (weak coupling, asymptotic freedom)
    

This is **asymptotic freedom** — the subject of the 2004 Nobel Prize in Physics. The prediction that the strong interaction weakens at higher energies was a major revelation for the physics community at the time.

### **11.3 Higgs Boson Production and Decay**

In the simulation, the Higgs boson is produced with a low probability of about 10⁻⁵ when √s ≥ 8,000 GeV. The primary production channels are:

- gg → H (gluon fusion, the most important channel at the LHC)
    
- qq̄ → H (quark-antiquark annihilation)
    

H⁰ decay branching ratios (PDG 2022):

- H → bb̄: 58.2% (dominant)
    
- H → WW*: 21.4%
    
- H → gg: 8.2%
    
- H → τ+τ-: 6.3%
    
- H → ZZ*: 2.6%
    
- H → γγ: 0.23% (rare, but an important detection channel)
    

### **11.4 Neutrino Oscillations**

This simulation also implements neutrino oscillations (NeutrinoOscillation.oscillate()).

Survival probability (2-flavor approximation): `P(νμ→νμ) = 1 - sin²(2θ) sin²(1.27 Δm² L/E)`

PDG 2022 values:

- Atmospheric neutrinos: Δm²_atm = 2.5×10⁻³ eV², sin²(2θ₂₃) ≈ 1.0 (θ₂₃ ≈ 45°)
    
- Solar neutrinos: Δm²_sol = 7.4×10⁻⁵ eV², sin²(2θ₁₂) = 0.846 (θ₁₂ ≈ 34°)
    

This oscillation phenomenon was the key concept that resolved the solar neutrino problem, proving the existence of new physics beyond the Standard Model (neutrino mass).

---

## **12. CP Violation and B Meson Oscillations**

The simulation also implements CP violation and B⁰ meson oscillations.

### **12.1 CP Violation Parameters**

The CP-violating phase of the CKM matrix is expressed by the Jarlskog invariant: J ≈ 3.0×10⁻⁵

B⁰-B̄⁰ mixing is described by: `B⁰(t) = e^(-iMt - Γt/2) [cos(Δmt/2)|B⁰⟩ - i sin(Δmt/2)|B̄⁰⟩]`

PDG 2022 values:

- Δm_d = 0.5065×10¹² ℏ s⁻¹ (oscillation frequency)
    

### **12.2 Measurement of sin(2β)**

The time-dependent CP asymmetry observed in B⁰ → J/ψ K⁰ decays is: `A_CP(t) = -sin(2β) sin(Δm_d t)`

The world average of sin(2β) is 0.699 ± 0.017 (PDG 2022).

The simulation’s CPViolation.asymmetry(t) function: `return sin2beta × sin(delta_m × t_ps)`

This correctly implements the actual formula. This measurement, which proved the existence of matter-antimatter asymmetry, was an important discovery; Kobayashi and Maskawa received the 2008 Nobel Prize in Physics for their theoretical prediction of this effect.

---

## **13. Synthesis: The Complete Physical Picture the Simulation Depicts**

### **13.1 Reinterpretation of the Physical Situation**

From my perspective, this simulation depicts roughly the following physical situation:

> **A system in which, around a Kerr black hole with a strong magnetic field (B ~ 10¹²-10¹⁵ G), a GRB/AGN-level ultra-high-energy environment (√s ~ 10²⁴ GeV) is established; Standard Model particles are generated by this environment; a competition between the magnetic field and gravity forms an accretion disk; charge separation isolates matter from antimatter; and the black hole’s effective mass changes dynamically as energy accumulates**

### **13.2 Physical Causes of the Observed Phenomena — Summary**

**Phenomenon 1: Spontaneous formation of an accretion disk**

- Cause: Particles are captured at an effective ISCO where the magnetic field overpowers gravity
    
- Related theory: Kerr metric ISCO + MHD magnetic pressure
    

**Phenomenon 2: Charge-dependent symmetric separation**

- Cause: Sign dependence of the Lorentz force F=qv×B
    
- Related theory: Cyclotron motion, Goldreich-Julian charge separation
    

**Phenomenon 3: Mass-dependent orbital radius separation**

- Cause: Larmor radius r_L = γmv⊥/(|q|B) ∝ m
    
- Related theory: Cyclotron mass separation, a kind of “magnetic spectrometer” effect
    

**Phenomenon 4: Annihilation suppression**

- Cause: Charge separation spatially isolates matter and antimatter
    
- Related theory: Charge separation in strong magnetic fields, matter-antimatter domain formation
    

**Phenomenon 5: Dynamic mass and gravity variations**

- Cause: Equivalent mass contribution from energy accumulation via E=mc²
    
- Related theory: Einstein field equations, mass rate-of-change equation (dM/dt), QPO
    

### **13.3 Correspondence with Real Astrophysical Objects**

I thought about which real celestial objects most closely resemble the system this simulation depicts:

**Magnetar**

- B ~ 10¹⁴-10¹⁵ G (the strongest magnetic fields in the universe)
    
- Extreme charge separation phenomena
    
- A magnetosphere where the magnetic field overwhelmingly overpowers gravity
    

**Gamma-Ray Burst (GRB)**

- The most energetic explosive events in the universe
    
- Accretion disk formed immediately after black hole creation
    
- Relativistic jet (Γ ~ 100-1000)
    

**Active Galactic Nucleus (AGN)**

- Supermassive black hole (10⁶-10¹⁰ M☉)
    
- Accretion disk + relativistic jet
    
- Birthplace of high-energy cosmic rays
    

**Black Hole X-ray Binary**

- Observable QPO (quasi-periodic oscillations)
    
- Trackable dynamic changes in the accretion disk
    
- Measurable frame-dragging effects

## **14. Unsolved Problems — The Grand Questions This Simulation Encounters**

Through this simulation I have observed many phenomena and drawn connections, but at the same time I have encountered some of the most fundamental unsolved problems in physics.

These are not evidence that the simulation is “wrong” — rather, I think they show **that even this small simulation can be connected to the deepest questions in physics.**

**The Matter-Antimatter Asymmetry Problem**

- Our current understanding is that CP violation alone cannot fully explain the observed predominance of matter
    
- Thinking about what role the charge-separation mechanism seen in this simulation may have played in the early universe is a fascinating topic
    

**The Origin of Ultra-High-Energy Cosmic Rays (UHECR)**

- Particles with energies exceeding the GZK limit (~5×10¹⁹ eV) are observed
    
- The environment depicted in this simulation (AGN, GRB) is one of the leading candidates as their source
    

**The Black Hole Information Paradox**

- Does Hawking radiation destroy information, or preserve it?
    
- The famous debate between Stephen Hawking, Leonard Susskind, and Gerard ‘t Hooft
    

**Quantum Gravity**

- At the Planck scale (10¹⁹ GeV), how is spacetime described quantum mechanically?
    
- Several approaches — string theory, loop quantum gravity, and others — are currently competing
    

**A Complete MHD Simulation of the Accretion Disk**

- Full GRMHD (General-Relativistic MagnetoHydroDynamics) code development is actively ongoing
    
- This simulation can be seen as one of the most simplified models among them
    

**A Complete Theoretical Explanation of QPOs**

- Multiple models are competing: parametric resonance, Lense-Thirring precession, fluid resonance, and others
    
- No single theory that fully matches observations has yet been established
    

**The Detailed Physics of Jet Formation**

- What is the relative contribution of the BZ (Blandford-Znajek) and BP (Blandford-Payne) mechanisms?
    
- Which is dominant in real astrophysical objects is still not clear

# **[Appendix] Dynamic Black Hole Mass and Independent Mass Generation Mechanisms**

_This appendix is designed to be read alongside Delayed Gravitational Interaction as a Mechanism for Spiral Arm Formation in Disk Galaxies (B. Sun, Seoul Inside, 2026), as a follow-up to the Yang-Mills Collider v3.2 + Kerr BH simulation analysis document._

---

## **A. Introduction: The Premises of Existing Theory and Their Limitations**

In the preceding sections, we examined the spacetime structure around a Kerr black hole, particle motion, energy-mass equivalence, and dynamic changes in gravity. In this appendix, I would like to explore one extended possibility that naturally emerges from that analysis.

The core premises of existing black hole physics are roughly as follows:

- Increases in a black hole’s mass occur only through the infall of external matter (accretion)
    
- Black holes are essentially stationary at the centers of galaxies
    
- Gravitational propagation is treated as instantaneous, or as unrelated to dynamic mass changes
    

Yet the phenomena observed in the Yang-Mills Collider v3.2 simulation seemed to raise new questions about these premises. What was particularly interesting was the possibility that the ultra-high-energy environment around the black hole could directly create matter from the vacuum, and that this might change the black hole’s effective mass independently of external accretion.

---

## **B. The Fundamental Difference Between the Solar System and a Galaxy: Stability of the Gravitational Origin**

### **B.1 The Solar System**

In the solar system, 99.8% of the total mass is concentrated in a single body — the Sun. As a result:

- The gravitational origin is essentially fixed
    
- The direction and magnitude of gravity felt by the planets are predictable
    
- Kepler’s laws hold to high precision
    
- The effect of gravitational propagation delay on orbital dynamics is negligible
    

### **B.2 The Galaxy**

In a galaxy, however, the situation is fundamentally different. Mass is distributed among hundreds of billions of stars, and the central black hole moves reactively within that distributed gravitational field.

`M_galaxy = M_BH + M_stars + M_gas + M_dark(?)`

Here, M_BH constitutes only 0.1–0.5% of the total galactic mass. Therefore, the black hole is not the dominant gravitational source of the galaxy — it is closer to a single body moving within a distributed gravitational field.

A summary of the key differences between the two systems:

- Category / Solar System / Galaxy
    
- Mass distribution / Centrally concentrated (99.8%) / Distributed (BH < 0.1%)
    
- Gravitational origin / Fixed / Dynamic (BH moves)
    
- Kepler’s laws / Holds / Deviations occur (rotation curve problem)
    
- Spiral structure / None / Naturally arises (when offset)
    

---

## **C. Three Mechanisms for Black Hole Gravitational Variability**

Three independent pathways by which the black hole’s gravity may not be static can be considered. These mechanisms are not mutually exclusive and may operate simultaneously in real environments.

### **C.1 Mechanism 1: Phase Desynchronization Between Black Hole Motion and Galactic Rotation**

When the black hole is not perfectly stationary at the galactic center but is offset, and gravity propagates at a finite speed:

`r_BH(t) ≠ r_galaxy_center(t)`

`F_gravity ∝ G·M_BH / |r_star - r_BH(t - r/c)|²`

The gravitational origin felt by stars is not the black hole’s current position, but its past position. This discrepancy creates a tangential force component.

Results verified in the galaxy simulator:

- BH offset = 0 condition: no spiral arm formation (symmetric disk)
    
- BH offset > 0 condition: spontaneous spiral arm formation (100% reproduced)
    
- After sufficient time: spiral arm disappearance (confirming the dynamic nature of the structure)
    

This implies that spiral arms are a transient imprint of the black hole’s offset. I thought this was a very interesting mechanism that simultaneously explains both formation and dissolution.

**Prediction:** Any galaxy with spiral arms will necessarily have an offset black hole. This is a falsifiable prediction verifiable with current technology.

### **C.2 Mechanism 2: Independent Mass Generation Through Pair Production in Ultra-High-Energy Environments**

The magnetic field environment around a black hole is on a scale that makes comparison to humanity’s most powerful particle accelerator meaningless:

- LHC (humanity’s strongest): B=8.3 T, energy scale 13,000 GeV
    
- Magnetar: B~10¹⁴ T (10¹⁰ times!), energy scale ~10²⁰ GeV
    
- AGN black hole: B~10¹⁰⁻¹¹ T (10⁹ times!), energy scale ~10²⁴ GeV and above
    

In this environment, the following chain of pair production – isolation – accumulation can occur:

`γ + γ → e⁺ + e⁻` (or heavier particle-antiparticle pairs) `B >> B_critical` → annihilation suppressed (`r_L << R_system`) Matter + antimatter → spatial isolation → gravitational contribution maintained

The key insight here is: **antimatter also has gravity.** By the equivalence principle, matter and antimatter have identical gravitational properties, and if a pair-produced pair exists in isolation without annihilating, both contribute as gravitational sources.

This requires adding a new term to the conventional dM/dt equation:

`dM/dt = Ṁ_in + Ṁ_pair - Ṁ_out - Ṗ_Hawking/c²`

where Ṁ_pair is the independent mass contribution rate from pair production. To my knowledge, this term has not been explicitly treated in the existing literature.

Three possible fates for the isolated matter and antimatter:

- When the magnetic field weakens, annihilation → energy release → gravity decreases (creating variability)
    
- Absorbed by the black hole → incorporated as mass → gravity increases
    
- Ejected as a jet → moves to the galactic outskirts → gravity redistributed
    

Any of these pathways creates gravitational variability in the black hole. This is the core of Mechanism 2.

### **C.3 Mechanism 3: Gravitational Propagation and the Event Horizon (A Separate Claim)**

This mechanism is qualitatively different from the previous two. Because it is a question about the foundations of physics, I flag it separately.

If gravity propagates at the speed of light, a tension arises in a black hole whose mass is dynamically changing:

- Light has no mass, yet is affected by gravity and cannot escape the horizon
    
- Gravitational waves also propagate at the speed of light
    
- Therefore, gravitational wave signals arising from dynamic mass changes should also logically be unable to escape the horizon
    

General relativity (GR) sidesteps this problem with static solutions (Schwarzschild, Kerr). Because these are time-independent solutions, the propagation delay problem does not arise.

However, for a black hole whose mass is dynamically changing via accretion, this workaround may not hold. This is a region where GR currently does not provide a clear answer.

**Note:** This claim is independent of Mechanisms 1 and 2. Mechanism 3 is not required for 1 and 2 to hold. It is a separate problem requiring deeper theoretical investigation.

---

## **D. The Possibility of Independent Mass Generation by Black Holes**

### **D.1 Limitations of the Existing Paradigm**

All current astronomical explanations for black hole mass growth depend on external infall:

- Absorption of neighboring stars (tidal disruption events, TDE)
    
- Gas accretion (continuous infall through the accretion disk)
    
- Galaxy mergers (merger of two black holes)
    

What all these mechanisms have in common is that the black hole receives matter from its surroundings. The perspective that the black hole itself generates matter does not exist in the existing literature.

### **D.2 An Unsolved Problem: Supermassive Black Holes in the Early Universe**

One of the most serious unsolved problems in current astronomy is this:

> How could supermassive black holes with masses of about 10 billion solar masses (~10⁹ M☉) have grown so rapidly in the early universe (z > 6, within the first billion years of cosmic history)?

Under the Eddington luminosity limit, reaching this mass through external accretion alone requires the Eddington time (approximately 450 million years):

`t_Eddington = M / Ṁ_Edd = 450 Myr` (for radiative efficiency η=0.1)

Yet the observed quasars reached this mass in far less time. External accretion alone is woefully insufficient in terms of time.

### **D.3 A Proposed Independent Mass Generation Mechanism**

Applying the logic of Mechanism 2 to the early universe opens up a new possibility:

- The environment around black holes in the early universe was far more extreme than today
    
- Higher energy density → higher pair-production rate
    
- Stronger magnetic fields → more efficient annihilation suppression
    
- There may have been conditions where the Ṁ_pair term could dominate over Ṁ_in
    

If this mechanism operated, the problem of rapid supermassive black hole growth in the early universe would be naturally resolved. The black hole would not be “eating” its surroundings — it would have a structure that converts its own energy (spin, magnetic field) into mass.

`E_spin = M - M_irr ≤ 0.29 Mc²` (extractable rotational energy)

`Ṁ_pair ∝ B² × (dV/dt)_ergosphere × η_pair`

However, from the perspective of energy conservation, this process comes at the cost of a reduction in the black hole’s spin energy. Whether this leads to a net increase in mass requires additional quantitative investigation.

---

## **E. Connection to the Dark Matter Problem**

If Mechanism 2 operates at a meaningful scale, it may offer a partial explanation for the galactic rotation curve problem.

### **E.1 Distribution of Isolated Matter-Antimatter**

Matter and antimatter pair-produced and isolated near a black hole are difficult to observe electromagnetically. They are isolated by strong magnetic fields and are not cold enough to be captured by ordinary observational channels.

However, they clearly possess mass and exert gravity. That is:

> **It is possible that some of what we call dark matter is actually ordinary matter and antimatter that has accumulated in isolation near a black hole.**

### **E.2 What This Hypothesis Can Explain**

- The tendency for dark matter to be concentrated near galactic centers → consistent with the pair-production environment around the black hole
    
- Persistent failure of direct dark matter detection experiments → merely Standard Model particles in a special state (isolated)
    
- The varying dark matter fraction from galaxy to galaxy → explainable by differences in black hole spin and magnetic field environment
    

### **E.3 Limitations**

Of course, this hypothesis alone cannot explain everything. The dark matter distribution in the outer regions of galaxies is particularly difficult to explain by this mechanism alone. A complete explanation may require combining it with additional mechanisms such as the annihilation gravitational remnant (discussed in Appendix B).

---

## **F. Synthesis: Interrelationships Among the Three Mechanisms and Paths to Verification**

### **F.1 Strength Spectrum of the Claims**

A summary of the claim strength and verification status of each mechanism:

**Mechanism 1 (BH offset)**

- Claim strength: Strong
    
- Verification method: Galaxy simulator (100% reproduced)
    
- Current status: Simulation verification complete
    

**Mechanism 2 (Pair-production mass)**

- Claim strength: Intermediate
    
- Verification method: Measurement of pair-production rates around AGN
    
- Current status: Hypothesis (quantification required)
    

**Mechanism 3 (Gravitational propagation limit)**

- Claim strength: Separate
    
- Verification method: Gravitational wave observations of dynamic BHs
    
- Current status: Stage of theoretical investigation
    

### **F.2 The Full Chain of Logic**

Summarizing the chain of logic from the Yang-Mills simulator to galactic scales:

Black hole ultra-high-energy environment ↓ Pair production + magnetic isolation → Ṁ_pair > 0 ↓ Dynamic change in effective black hole mass (M(t) ≠ const) ↓ Gravitational propagation delay + BH offset ↓ Tangential force component arises ↓ Spontaneous spiral arm formation + rotation curve deviations

Each step is grounded in established principles of existing physics. This is not the introduction of new physics — it is **existing physics connected in a new sequence.**

### **F.3 Future Research Directions**

- Quantitative estimation of Ṁ_pair: analysis of the relationship between magnetic field strength and pair-production rate in AGN environments
    
- Advancement of galaxy simulator: real-time incorporation of dynamic BH mass changes
    
- Proposed observational program: precision measurement of BH positions in spiral galaxies
    
- Early universe application: comparison of z > 6 quasar growth rates with Ṁ_pair estimates
    
- Dark matter alternative investigation: distribution and observational signatures of isolated pair-produced matter
    

---

## **G. Conclusion**

The core claim presented in this appendix can be summarized in one statement:

> **A black hole is not a static mass concentration fixed at the center of a galaxy. It is a dynamic system that moves, generates mass on its own, and propagates its gravity at a finite speed. The combination of these three facts can be a new starting point for explaining spiral arms, rotation curve deviations, and the rapid growth of supermassive black holes in the early universe.**

These claims may sound radical, but each step is grounded in textbook-level physics. What is radical is not the individual steps, but **the way they are connected.**

And that connection did not start from theory — it is **the result of observing in a simulator and tracing back the implications.**

# **Delayed Gravitational Interaction as a Mechanism for Spiral Arm Formation in Disk Galaxies**

## **About This Document**

This document was written by a non-specialist.

It is not a peer-reviewed paper. It is a structured record of an investigation — conducted through simulation, observation of emergent behavior, and systematic reasoning — into a specific question: whether the finite propagation speed of gravity, combined with black hole displacement, is sufficient to produce spiral arm structure in disk galaxies without invoking dark matter or density wave theory.

The answer suggested by the simulations described here is yes. That answer is presented carefully, with its limitations stated explicitly.

Three claims are made. They are independent of one another. The first is directly verifiable by any reader with a web browser. The second and third are hypotheses — stated as possibilities, not conclusions.

---

## **Three Claims**

The following three claims structure this document. They are stated here, at the outset, without elaboration. Each is developed in full in the sections that follow.

---

**Claim I.** When a black hole is displaced from the geometric center of a disk galaxy — however slightly — spiral arm structure emerges immediately and persistently. This is a consequence of the finite propagation speed of gravity: stars at different distances from the black hole reference different past positions of that black hole, producing a systematic angular offset in the gravitational force vector that, in a rotating system, maps onto a spiral pattern. This claim is verifiable directly. Open the simulator linked above. Press any arrow key. Observe.

**Claim II.** The ultra-strong magnetic fields observed in the vicinity of supermassive black holes — combined with the extreme energy densities of accretion disks and relativistic jets — create conditions approaching or exceeding the Schwinger critical field, at which spontaneous pair production from vacuum occurs. The magnetic field separates the produced particle and antiparticle in opposite directions, spatially suppressing annihilation. This process constitutes a mechanism for continuous local mass generation and destruction around black holes. The scale of this effect is unknown. It may be non-negligible. This claim is a hypothesis.

**Claim III.** If gravity propagates at a finite speed, then the gravitational signal itself is subject to the curvature of spacetime through which it travels. In regions of extreme curvature — near black holes — the gravitational signal from infalling mass may be redirected, delayed, or effectively trapped. This would mean that the gravitational influence of mass accreted beyond the event horizon is not fully communicated to the exterior. Observed black hole masses would then represent lower bounds on actual masses. This claim is a hypothesis, and the most speculative of the three. It is included because it connects structurally to Claims I and II, and because its implications — if correct — are significant enough to warrant explicit statement.

---

_These three claims are independent. Claim I does not require Claims II or III to be true. The strength of this document rests primarily on Claim I, which requires no theoretical commitment beyond the already-verified finite propagation speed of gravitational waves (Abbott et al., LIGO/Virgo, 2017)._

---

## **Section 1. The Spiral Arm Formation Mechanism**

---

[

![](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!Exi7!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Ff13ff3c7-d8fc-426c-a083-a217fa8584c0_1405x1027.png)



](https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!Exi7!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Ff13ff3c7-d8fc-426c-a083-a217fa8584c0_1405x1027.png)

### **1.1 The Problem with Existing Explanations**

Spiral arm structure is the dominant morphological feature of disk galaxies. Approximately 60–70% of observed galaxies in the local universe exhibit spiral structure (Lintott et al., 2011, Galaxy Zoo). The persistence and prevalence of this structure constitutes one of the oldest unsolved problems in galactic dynamics.

The standard theoretical framework — Lin-Shu density wave theory (Lin & Shu, 1964) — treats spiral arms as quasi-stationary density waves propagating through the stellar disk. The theory was motivated in part by the **winding problem**: in a differentially rotating disk, any material arm would be sheared into oblivion within a few galactic rotation periods (roughly 10⁸–10⁹ years). Since spiral arms persist on timescales far exceeding this, they cannot be material structures rotating with the stars. Density wave theory resolves this by treating the arm as a pattern — analogous to a traffic jam through which individual cars pass — rather than a fixed collection of stars.

However, Lin-Shu density wave theory carries its own unresolved difficulties:

1. **The pattern speed problem.** The theory requires a well-defined pattern speed Ω_p distinct from the local stellar angular velocity. Observational determination of pattern speeds is indirect and contested. A single, stable pattern speed is not consistently recovered across galaxy samples.
    
2. **The maintenance problem.** Density waves dissipate. Maintaining them against damping requires a continuous energy source — typically invoked as swing amplification or bar-driven resonance — whose generality is not established.
    
3. **The universality problem.** If density wave theory requires specific resonance conditions, the 60–70% prevalence of spiral structure is unexplained. Why should most disk galaxies independently satisfy the required conditions?
    
4. **The formation problem.** Density wave theory describes the propagation of an existing wave. It does not explain how the initial wave is established.
    

A second class of explanations invokes **tidal interactions** — gravitational perturbations from companion galaxies or satellite systems. Tidal spiral arms are well-documented in interacting pairs (e.g., M51/NGC 5195). However, isolated spiral galaxies without obvious interaction partners also exhibit persistent spiral structure, limiting the generality of tidal explanations.

The mechanism described in this document requires none of the above. It requires only two conditions, both of which hold universally in any physical galaxy:

1. Gravity propagates at a finite speed.
    
2. The central black hole is not at rest relative to the galactic disk.
    

Both conditions are empirically established facts, not model assumptions. The mechanism is therefore not a special-case explanation. It is a general one.

### **1.2 The Mechanism: Retarded Gravity and Black Hole Displacement**

#### **1.2.1 Finite Propagation Speed of Gravity**

The finite propagation speed of gravitational influence is not a novel hypothesis. It is a direct prediction of General Relativity, confirmed observationally by the detection of gravitational waves at the speed of light (Abbott et al., 2017, GW170817 + GRB 170817A; speed of gravity constrained to within 10⁻¹⁵ of _c_).

The consequence for galactic dynamics has received limited attention in the mainstream literature, with notable exceptions (Yahalom, 2013, 2019, 2024; Van Flandern, 1998 — though the latter’s conclusions are disputed). The argument is straightforward:

If gravitational influence propagates at _c_, then the gravitational force experienced by a star at distance _r_ from a black hole at time _t_ is determined not by the black hole’s position at _t_, but by its position at _t_ − _r/c_.

For a black hole at rest, this introduces no asymmetry: the retarded position equals the current position, and the force field is spherically symmetric. Spiral structure does not emerge.

For a black hole in motion — however slight — the retarded position differs from the current position by an amount proportional to both the velocity of the black hole and the light-travel time _r/c_. Stars at different distances therefore reference different past positions of the same black hole. The gravitational force vectors experienced by stars at different radii point in systematically different directions. In a rotating disk, this angular offset is sheared into a spiral pattern.

This is not a perturbative effect that requires large black hole velocities. It is a structural consequence of differential light-travel time across the disk. Even a black hole displaced by a fraction of a parsec from the disk center will produce a measurable angular offset in the force vectors of stars at kiloparsec distances.

#### **1.2.2 The Universality Argument**

The black hole in a real galaxy is never at rest. It cannot be. The following perturbations act continuously and simultaneously:

- The host galaxy moves through space (peculiar velocity typically 100–600 km/s).
    
- Satellite galaxies and globular clusters exert time-varying tidal forces.
    
- The galactic bar, when present, drives the black hole in a periodic orbit about the disk center.
    
- In merging systems, the two black holes orbit each other prior to coalescence.
    
- The large-scale structure of the universe — the cosmic web of filaments and voids — exerts a slowly varying gravitational background.
    

Perfect black hole stasis would require exact cancellation of all these perturbations simultaneously and continuously. This is not physically plausible. Black hole motion is the default state. Spiral structure is therefore the expected morphology for disk galaxies — not a special condition requiring explanation.

This directly addresses the universality problem: 60–70% spiral prevalence is not surprising if spiral structure is the generic outcome of black hole motion in a disk. The remaining 30–40% — ellipticals, lenticulars, irregulars — represent systems where disk structure itself is absent, disrupted, or dynamically suppressed.

---

### **1.3 Mathematical Formulation**

#### **1.3.1 Equation of Motion**

The full equation of motion for a star at position **r** in the galactic disk is:

d2rdt2=aN(r,t)+ar(r,t)_dt_2_d_2**r**​=**a**_N_​(**r**,_t_)+**a**_r_​(**r**,_t_)

where aN**a**_N_​ is the Newtonian gravitational acceleration from the disk and black hole (evaluated at current position), and ar**a**_r_​ is the retarded gravitational correction.

**Unicode:** d²r/dt² = a_N(r,t) + a_r(r,t)

**LaTeX:**

d2rdt2=aN(r,t)+ar(r,t)_dt_2_d_2**r**​=**a**_N_​(**r**,_t_)+**a**_r_​(**r**,_t_)

**Mathematica:**

```
r''[t] == aN[r[t], t] + ar[r[t], t]
```

**Python (SymPy):**

```
from sympy import *
t = symbols('t')
r = Function('r')
aN, ar = Function('aN'), Function('ar')
eq = Eq(r(t).diff(t,2), aN(r(t), t) + ar(r(t), t))
```

---

#### **1.3.2 Newtonian Component: Black Hole and Freeman Disk**

The Newtonian acceleration comprises contributions from the central black hole and the stellar disk:

aN=−GMBH∣r−rBH∣3(r−rBH)−GMdisk(r)r3r**a**_N_​=−∣**r**−**r**_BH_​∣3_GMBH_​​(**r**−**r**_BH_​)−_r_3_GMdisk_​(_r_)​**r**

The disk mass enclosed within radius _r_ follows the Freeman (1970) exponential surface density profile:

Mdisk(r)=Mtotal[1−(1+rRd)e−r/Rd]_Mdisk_​(_r_)=_Mtotal_​[1−(1+_Rd_​_r_​)_e_−_r_/_Rd_​]

where Rd_Rd_​ is the disk scale length and Mtotal_Mtotal_​ is the total disk mass.

**Unicode:** M_disk(r) = M_total × [1 − (1 + r/R_d) × exp(−r/R_d)]

**LaTeX:**

Mdisk(r)=Mtotal[1−(1+rRd)e−r/Rd]_Mdisk_​(_r_)=_Mtotal_​[1−(1+_Rd_​_r_​)_e_−_r_/_Rd_​]

**Mathematica:**

```
Mdisk[r_] := Mtotal*(1 - (1 + r/Rd)*Exp[-r/Rd])
```

**Python (SymPy):**

```
from sympy import symbols, exp
r, Rd, Mtotal = symbols('r R_d M_total', positive=True)
Mdisk = Mtotal * (1 - (1 + r/Rd) * exp(-r/Rd))
```

---

#### **1.3.3 Retarded Gravitational Correction**

Following Yahalom (2013, 2019, 2024), the retarded gravitational acceleration in the point-mass approximation is:

ar=α⋅GM¨BH2c2R^**a**_r_​=_α_⋅2_c_2_GM_¨_BH_​​_R_^

where:

- α_α_ is a dimensionless retardation strength parameter (0 ≤ α ≤ 10 in the simulator; α = 1 corresponds to the physical prediction)
    
- M¨BH=d2MBH/dt2_M_¨_BH_​=_d_2_MBH_​/_dt_2 is the second time derivative of the black hole mass
    
- R^_R_^ is the unit vector from black hole to star
    
- _c_ is the speed of light
    

**The critical structural feature** of this term is the absence of an inverse-distance dependence. Unlike Newtonian gravity (∝1/r2∝1/_r_2) or even the leading post-Newtonian corrections, the retarded term ar**a**_r_​ does not diminish with distance. This is directly analogous to the radiation zone of an accelerating charge in electromagnetism, where the radiation field falls as 1/_r_ rather than 1/_r_². At galactic scales, this means that retarded gravitational effects become _relatively_ more important at large radii — precisely the regime where observed rotation curves deviate most strongly from Newtonian predictions.

**Unicode:** a_r = α × G × M_BH’‘ / (2c²) × R_hat

**LaTeX:**

ar=α⋅GM¨BH2c2R^**a**_r_​=_α_⋅2_c_2_GM_¨_BH_​​_R_^

**Mathematica:**

```
ar = alpha * G * MBHdd / (2 * c^2) * Rhat
(* MBHdd = second time derivative of BH mass *)
```

**Python (SymPy):**

```
from sympy import symbols, Function
t, alpha, G, c = symbols('t alpha G c', positive=True)
MBH = Function('MBH')
MBHdd = MBH(t).diff(t, 2)
ar = alpha * G * MBHdd / (2 * c**2)  # scalar magnitude; multiply by unit vector R_hat
```

---

#### **1.3.4 Retarded Position and the History Buffer**

The retarded position of the black hole — the position seen by a star at distance _r_ at time _t_ — is:

rBHret(t,r)=rBH(t−α⋅rc)**r**_BHret_​(_t_,_r_)=**r**_BH_​(_t_−_cα_⋅_r_​)

In the simulator, this is computed by maintaining a first-in, first-out (FIFO) position history buffer for the black hole, with a maximum depth of 300 steps. For each star, the appropriate past position is recovered by linear interpolation:

rBH(t−τ)≈rBH(t0)+τ−t0t1−t0[rBH(t1)−rBH(t0)]**r**_BH_​(_t_−_τ_)≈**r**_BH_​(_t_0​)+_t_1​−_t_0​_τ_−_t_0​​[**r**_BH_​(_t_1​)−**r**_BH_​(_t_0​)]

where t0_t_0​ and t1_t_1​ are the two buffer entries bracketing the target time t−τ_t_−_τ_, and τ=αr/c_τ_=_αr_/_c_.

**This interpolation is the mechanism that produces spiral arms.**

Each star pulls its gravitational force vector toward a different past position of the black hole. Stars closer to the center reference a more recent position; stars at large radii reference an older position. When the black hole is in motion, these past positions are spatially distinct. The resulting force vectors point in systematically different directions as a function of radius. In a differentially rotating disk, this radially-dependent angular offset is sheared continuously into an Archimedean spiral pattern.

The geometry is exact and deterministic. No stochastic process, no resonance condition, no special initial configuration is required. The spiral is a direct geometric consequence of differential light-travel time across a rotating disk around a displaced massive object.

**Unicode:** τ = α × r / c r_BH_ret(t, r) = r_BH(t − τ)

**LaTeX:**

τ=αrc,rBHret(t,r)=rBH!(t−αrc)_τ_=_cαr_​,**r**_BHret_​(_t_,_r_)=**r**_BH_​!(_t_−_cαr_​)

**Mathematica:**

```
tau[r_] := alpha * r / c
rBHret[t_, r_] := rBHhistory[t - tau[r]]
(* rBHhistory interpolated from stored buffer *)
```

**Python (SymPy / NumPy):**

```
import numpy as np

def get_retarded_position(bh_history, times, t_now, r, alpha, c):
    tau = alpha * r / c
    t_target = t_now - tau
    # linear interpolation from history buffer
    idx = np.searchsorted(times, t_target)
    if idx == 0:
        return bh_history[0]
    t0, t1 = times[idx-1], times[idx]
    r0, r1 = bh_history[idx-1], bh_history[idx]
    frac = (t_target - t0) / (t1 - t0)
    return r0 + frac * (r1 - r0)
```

### **1.4 The Leapfrog (Velocity Verlet) Integrator**

The simulator uses a leapfrog integrator (equivalent to velocity Verlet) for time evolution. This choice is not arbitrary.

vn+1/2=vn+a(rn)Δt2_vn_+1/2​=_vn_​+_a_(**r**_n_​)2Δ_t_​

rn+1=rn+vn+1/2,Δt**r**_n_+1​=**r**_n_​+_vn_+1/2​,Δ_t_

an+1=a(rn+1)_an_+1​=_a_(**r**_n_+1​)

vn+1=vn+1/2+an+1Δt2_vn_+1​=_vn_+1/2​+_an_+1​2Δ_t_​

Properties relevant to this application:

PropertyValueSignificanceFormal accuracyO(Δt²)Sufficient for galactic dynamics timescalesTime reversibilityYesEssential for distinguishing physical behavior from integration artifactsSymplecticityYesPreserves phase space volume; prevents artificial energy drift over long integrationsEnergy conservationNear-exactΔE/E monitored in real time in simulatorAngular momentum conservationNear-exactΔL/L monitored in real time in simulator

The real-time conservation diagnostics in GalaxyCS v4 are critical for the validity of the simulation results. If the observed spiral structure were an artifact of numerical instability, it would manifest as unbounded growth in ΔE or ΔL. The structure is observed to persist under conditions of stable energy and angular momentum conservation. This rules out the most common class of numerical artifacts.

**LaTeX:**

vn+1/2=vn+a(rn)Δt2,rn+1=rn+vn+1/2Δt,vn+1=vn+1/2+a(rn+1)Δt2_vn_+1/2​=_vn_​+**a**(**r**_n_​)2Δ_t_​,**r**_n_+1​=**r**_n_​+_vn_+1/2​Δ_t_,_vn_+1​=_vn_+1/2​+**a**(**r**_n_+1​)2Δ_t_​

**Mathematica:**

```
(* Velocity Verlet step *)
vHalf = v + a[r] * dt/2;
rNew = r + vHalf * dt;
vNew = vHalf + a[rNew] * dt/2;
```

**Python (NumPy):**

```
def leapfrog_step(r, v, a_func, dt):
    v_half = v + a_func(r) * dt / 2
    r_new = r + v_half * dt
    v_new = v_half + a_func(r_new) * dt / 2
    return r_new, v_new
```

---

### **1.5 Observed Behavior and Predicted Consequences**

#### **1.5.1 Immediate Spiral Formation**

In GalaxyCS v4, with a stellar population of 20,000–80,000 test particles initialized on circular orbits consistent with the Freeman disk rotation curve:

- With BH at rest (displacement = 0): no spiral structure. Axisymmetric disk.
    
- With BH displaced by any nonzero amount: spiral arm structure emerges within the first few simulation steps and persists indefinitely.
    
- The transition is not gradual. It is immediate. There is no critical displacement threshold below which the effect is absent.
    

This behavior is consistent with the mathematical analysis in Section 1.3.4: the spiral is a geometric consequence of differential retarded positions, not a resonance phenomenon with a threshold.

#### **1.5.2 Arm Count and Morphology**

The number and pitch angle of the spiral arms is determined by the kinematic history of the black hole displacement:

- Simple unidirectional displacement → two-arm spiral
    
- Oscillatory displacement → multi-arm structure
    
- Irregular displacement → flocculent or asymmetric arms
    

This predicts a direct correspondence between black hole kinematics and spiral morphology that is in principle observationally testable. The inverse problem — inferring black hole kinematic history from spiral morphology — is a novel observational tool suggested by this framework.

#### **1.5.3 Rotation Curve Behavior**

The HUD in GalaxyCS v4 displays, in real time:

- Observed stellar rotation velocity at outer disk radius
    
- Newtonian prediction for the same radius given the disk mass distribution
    
- The ratio (observed / predicted)
    

In runs with active retarded gravity and BH displacement, the outer rotation velocity consistently exceeds the Newtonian prediction by factors of 1.5–3.0, depending on the α parameter and BH displacement. This qualitatively reproduces the flat or rising rotation curves observed in disk galaxies — the primary observational motivation for dark matter.

The retarded gravitational term (Section 1.3.3) provides a natural explanation: because ar**a**_r_​ does not fall off with distance, it contributes increasingly to the total acceleration at large radii, sustaining circular velocities above the Newtonian prediction.

A quantitative comparison with the SPARC database (175 galaxies with resolved rotation curves; Lelli, McGaugh & Schombert, 2016) is identified as the primary near-term validation target (see Appendix H).

#### **1.5.4 Void Formation**

Reduction of the black hole mass parameter — simulating either mass loss, observation uncertainty, or temporary disruption of the effective gravitational influence — followed by displacement and mass recovery produces large underdense regions (voids) in the stellar distribution. This behavior emerges without any additional mechanism. The void is the complement of the overdense arms: stellar material evacuated from one region accumulates in another, driven by the time-varying retarded force field.

The structural correspondence between simulated voids and observed cosmic void distributions is noted but not claimed as a direct explanation. It is identified as a topic for further investigation.

---

### **1.6 Relationship to Existing Work**

The retarded gravity approach to galactic dynamics has been developed most systematically by Asher Yahalom (Ariel University). Key references:

- Yahalom, A. (2013). “Lorentz Symmetry Group, Retardation, Intergalactic Mass Depletion and Mechanisms Leading to Galactic Rotation Curves.” _Symmetry_, 5, 1–24.
    
- Yahalom, A. (2019). “Retardation Effects in Electromagnetism and Gravitation.” _Materials Today: Proceedings_, 14, 164–173.
    
- Yahalom, A. (2024). “The Cosmological Implication of Retarded Gravity.” (preprint)
    

The present work differs from Yahalom’s in several respects:

1. Yahalom’s analytical framework treats the mass distribution as continuous and derives corrections perturbatively. The present work uses direct N-body simulation with explicit position history buffers.
    
2. Yahalom does not emphasize black hole displacement as the primary driver of spiral arm formation. The present work places this at the center of the mechanism.
    
3. The present work connects the galactic-scale retarded gravity effect to particle-scale pair production dynamics via a second independent simulation (Yang-Mills Collider v3.2). This cross-scale connection is original to the present work.
    

The present work does not dispute Yahalom’s results. It extends them in a specific direction.

---

## **Section 2. Black Hole Mass and the Limits of Observation**

---

### **2.1 How Black Hole Masses Are Measured**

Current observational methods for determining black hole masses fall into four categories:

**1. Stellar dynamics.** Stars near the galactic center are tracked individually. Their orbital parameters (semi-major axis, period, eccentricity) determine the enclosed mass via Kepler’s third law. This method is applied with highest precision to Sgr A* at the center of the Milky Way (Ghez et al., 2008; Gillessen et al., 2009; GRAVITY Collaboration, 2019), yielding M≈4.15×106,M⊙_M_≈4.15×106,_M_⊙​.

**2. Gas dynamics.** The rotational velocity of gas in the galactic nucleus, combined with a model of the gravitational potential, yields an estimate of the central mass. Applicable to external galaxies where individual stellar orbits cannot be resolved.

**3. Reverberation mapping.** In active galactic nuclei (AGN), the time delay between continuum flux variations and the response of the broad-line region yields the size of that region. Combined with gas velocity measured via Doppler broadening, the virial theorem gives an estimate of MBH_MBH_​.

**4. Event horizon imaging.** The angular diameter of the black hole shadow — the photon capture cross-section — is proportional to the Schwarzschild radius and hence to the mass. Applied to M87* (Event Horizon Telescope Collaboration, 2019) and Sgr A* (EHT Collaboration, 2022).

All four methods share a structural feature: **they measure the gravitational influence of mass on surrounding matter or photons in the exterior spacetime.** None of them measures the interior of the black hole.

---

### **2.2 What These Methods Actually Measure**

Each method recovers a quantity of the form:

Mobs=v2rG(virial/Kepler approximation)_Mobs_​=_Gv_2_r_​(virial/Kepler approximation)

or more precisely, for the geodesic of a test particle in the Schwarzschild metric:

Mobs=c22Grs_Mobs_​=2_Gc_2​_rs_​

where rs_rs_​ is the Schwarzschild radius inferred from orbital or imaging data.

**Unicode:** M_obs = v² × r / G (virial) M_obs = c² × r_s / (2G) (Schwarzschild)

**LaTeX:**

Mobs=v2rG,Mobs=c2rs2G_Mobs_​=_Gv_2_r_​,_Mobs_​=2_Gc_2_rs_​​

**Mathematica:**

```
Mobs = v^2 * r / G
Mobs = c^2 * rs / (2*G)
```

**Python (SymPy):**

```
from sympy import symbols
v, r, G, c, rs = symbols('v r G c r_s', positive=True)
Mobs_virial = v**2 * r / G
Mobs_schwarzschild = c**2 * rs / (2*G)
```

In all cases, Mobs_Mobs_​ is the mass that produces the observed exterior gravitational field. It is not necessarily the total mass-energy contained within the black hole. The distinction matters only if the exterior gravitational field is not the full gravitational signature of the interior — which is the question raised in Section 2.3.

---

### **2.3 The Gravitational Horizon Problem**

General Relativity gives a precise and internally consistent answer to the question of whether a black hole’s gravity is detectable outside the event horizon: yes, fully and completely. The standard argument proceeds as follows.

The exterior Schwarzschild metric:

ds2=−(1−rsr)c2,dt2+(1−rsr)−1dr2+r2,dΩ2_ds_2=−(1−_rrs_​​)_c_2,_dt_2+(1−_rrs_​​)−1_dr_2+_r_2,_d_Ω2

is determined entirely by the mass parameter _M_, with no dependence on the interior structure. This is the content of Birkhoff’s theorem: the exterior vacuum solution is unique given the total mass. The gravitational field of a black hole in the exterior region is the same as that of any spherically symmetric mass distribution of the same total mass.

The standard resolution to the apparent paradox — “if nothing escapes the event horizon, how does the gravity escape?” — is that gravity does not “escape” in the sense that photons or massive particles escape. The gravitational field in the exterior is a property of the spacetime geometry that was established by the mass _as it fell in_, and the subsequent formation of the horizon does not erase that geometry. The geometry is already there.

This is a self-consistent description within classical GR.

**The present document does not claim that classical GR is wrong.**

The following observation is offered instead, more narrowly:

The classical GR description treats the spacetime as a fixed background onto which matter falls. In this picture, once the horizon forms, the exterior geometry is frozen. The mass that subsequently falls through the horizon does alter the metric — the horizon grows — but the change propagates outward at the speed of light via the gravitational signal of the infalling mass.

This means: **for mass accreted after horizon formation, the gravitational signal of that mass propagates outward at** _**c**_**.** If the accretion rate is high and the system is dynamically active — as in AGN and quasar-phase black holes — then at any given moment, a fraction of the recently accreted mass has not yet communicated its gravitational influence to the outer disk.

The magnitude of this effect depends on:

- The accretion rate M˙_M_˙
    
- The distance from the black hole to the stars in question
    
- The light-travel time r/c_r_/_c_
    

For a black hole accreting at the Eddington rate, the mass accreted in one light-crossing time of the galaxy (≈ 50,000 years for a 15 kpc galaxy) is:

ΔMEdd=M˙Edd⋅rc=LEddηc2⋅rcΔ_MEdd_​=_M_˙_Edd_​⋅_cr_​=_ηc_2_LEdd_​​⋅_cr_​

where η≈0.1_η_≈0.1 is the radiative efficiency. For MBH=108,M⊙_MBH_​=108,_M_⊙​ and r=15_r_=15 kpc:

M˙Edd≈2.2,M⊙,yr−1_M_˙_Edd_​≈2.2,_M_⊙​,yr−1

ΔM≈2.2×5×104,M⊙≈105,M⊙Δ_M_≈2.2×5×104,_M_⊙​≈105,_M_⊙​

This is small relative to 108,M⊙108,_M_⊙​. For quiescent black holes the effect is negligible.

For the earliest, most massive black holes — those at z>6_z_>6 with M>109,M⊙_M_>109,_M_⊙​ accreting near or above the Eddington limit — the effect may be more significant. **This is the regime where the observational anomaly described in Section 2.4 is most acute.**

**LaTeX:**

ΔMEdd=LEddηc2⋅rc,LEdd=4πGMBHmpcσTΔ_MEdd_​=_ηc_2_LEdd_​​⋅_cr_​,_LEdd_​=_σT_​4_πGMBH_​_mp_​_c_​

**Mathematica:**

```
LEdd[M_] := 4*Pi*G*M*mp*c/sigmaT
Mdot[M_] := LEdd[M] / (eta * c^2)
DeltaM[M_, r_] := Mdot[M] * r/c
```

**Python (SymPy):**

```
from sympy import pi, symbols
G, M, mp, c, sigmaT, eta, r = symbols('G M m_p c sigma_T eta r', positive=True)
LEdd = 4*pi*G*M*mp*c/sigmaT
Mdot_Edd = LEdd / (eta * c**2)
DeltaM = Mdot_Edd * r / c
```

---

### **2.4 The High-Redshift Black Hole Problem**

Observations from JWST (2022–present) have confirmed the existence of supermassive black holes with M>109,M⊙_M_>109,_M_⊙​ at redshifts z>6_z_>6, corresponding to a universe age of less than 900 million years (e.g., Larson et al., 2023; Bogdán et al., 2024).

The standard formation scenario — stellar-mass black hole seeds growing by Eddington-limited accretion — faces a severe timing problem. Starting from a stellar-mass seed (M0∼10,M⊙_M_0​∼10,_M_⊙​) and accreting continuously at the Eddington rate with η=0.1_η_=0.1:

M(t)=M0exp⁡!(ttEdd),tEdd=ησTc4πGmp≈450,Myr_M_(_t_)=_M_0​exp!(_tEdd_​_t_​),_tEdd_​=4_πGmp_​_ησT_​_c_​≈450,Myr

To reach 109,M⊙109,_M_⊙​ from 10,M⊙10,_M_⊙​:

t=tEddln⁡!(10910)=450×ln⁡(108),Myr≈450×18.4,Myr≈8.3,Gyr_t_=_tEdd_​ln!(10109​)=450×ln(108),Myr≈450×18.4,Myr≈8.3,Gyr

The universe at z=6_z_=6 is approximately 900 Myr old. The required growth time exceeds the available time by nearly an order of magnitude, even under idealized continuous Eddington accretion with no feedback.

Proposed resolutions include: heavy seeds from direct collapse black holes (DCBH), super-Eddington accretion episodes, or primordial black holes. None is without difficulty.

**The present document notes the following:** if observed black hole masses at high redshift represent lower bounds on actual masses — because (a) recently accreted mass has not fully communicated its gravitational influence to the observable tracers used in mass estimation, or (b) gravitational signals are attenuated in some way near the horizon — then the apparent growth problem is at least partially reframed. A black hole observed at z=6_z_=6 with an inferred mass of 109,M⊙109,_M_⊙​ might have an actual gravitational mass somewhat in excess of that value. The degree of excess is unknown and is not claimed to resolve the problem. It is offered as a structural observation.

**LaTeX:**

M(t)=M0exp⁡!(ttEdd),tEdd=ησTc4πGmp_M_(_t_)=_M_0​exp!(_tEdd_​_t_​),_tEdd_​=4_πGmp_​_ησT_​_c_​

**Mathematica:**

```
tEdd = eta * sigmaT * c / (4*Pi*G*mp)
M[t_] := M0 * Exp[t/tEdd]
tRequired = tEdd * Log[1*^9/10]  (* in Myr *)
```

**Python (SymPy):**

```
from sympy import symbols, exp, ln
t, t_Edd, M0 = symbols('t t_Edd M_0', positive=True)
M_t = M0 * exp(t / t_Edd)
# solve for t given M_t = 1e9 * M_solar, M0 = 10 * M_solar
t_required = t_Edd * ln(1e9 / 10)
```

---

### **2.5 Summary of Section 2**

This section makes no strong claims. It identifies a structural question — whether observed black hole masses are lower bounds — and traces its implications in two directions: the dynamical effect of accreted-but-not-yet-communicated mass on surrounding stars, and the high-redshift growth anomaly.

The honest statement of the position is:

> Observed black hole masses are measurements of the exterior gravitational field. The relationship between that measurement and the total mass-energy content of the black hole interior is not, in the general dynamical case, trivially one-to-one. The magnitude of the discrepancy, if any, is unknown. It may be negligible. It may not be.

This is sufficient motivation to treat observed black hole masses as lower bounds in simulation, which is what GalaxyCS v4 does when the BH mass slider is varied above its default value.

---

## **Section 3. Mass Generation in the Black Hole Environment**

---

### **3.1 Physical Conditions Near Supermassive Black Holes**

The environment immediately surrounding a supermassive black hole is not a vacuum in the physical sense. It is among the most energetically dense environments in the observable universe, characterized by three co-occurring extreme conditions:

**1. Ultra-strong magnetic fields.** Direct measurement of the magnetic field near M87* via polarimetric analysis of the Event Horizon Telescope data yields B∼1_B_∼1–3030 Gauss at the photon ring (EHT MWL Science Working Group, 2021). Extrapolating inward to the innermost stable circular orbit (ISCO) via flux-freezing arguments (B∝r−2_B_∝_r_−2 in the simplest case) gives estimates of 104104–106106 Gauss in the immediate vicinity of the horizon. Near neutron stars — which share some relevant magnetospheric physics — surface fields of 10121012–10151015 Gauss are routinely observed. The theoretical upper bound for black hole magnetospheres is poorly constrained.

**2. Extreme energy density.** The luminosity of AGN accretion disks approaches or exceeds the Eddington limit:

LEdd=4πGMcmpσT≈1.26×1038(MM⊙),erg/s_LEdd_​=_σT_​4_πGMcmp_​​≈1.26×1038(_M_⊙​_M_​),erg/s

For M=108,M⊙_M_=108,_M_⊙​, LEdd≈1046_LEdd_​≈1046 erg/s. The local energy density at the ISCO is:

u=L4πr2c_u_=4_πr_2_cL_​

For rISCO≈6,rs=6×2GMc2≈1013_rISCO_​≈6,_rs_​=6×_c_22_GM_​≈1013 cm (for 108,M⊙108,_M_⊙​):

u≈10464π×(1013)2×3×1010≈2.6×109,erg/cm3_u_≈4_π_×(1013)2×3×10101046​≈2.6×109,erg/cm3

**3. Relativistic jets and frame-dragging.** Spinning black holes (Kerr metric) drag the surrounding spacetime. In the ergosphere — the region between the static limit and the event horizon — the frame-dragging angular velocity:

ΩLT=2GJc2r3=acr3/rsΩ_LT_​=_c_2_r_32_GJ_​=_r_3/_rs_​_ac_​

where J=aGM2/c_J_=_aGM_2/_c_ is the angular momentum and a_a_ is the dimensionless spin parameter (0≤a≤10≤_a_≤1), becomes large enough to enforce co-rotation with the black hole on any locally stationary observer.

---

### **3.2 The Schwinger Mechanism: Pair Production from Vacuum**

In 1951, Julian Schwinger derived the rate of spontaneous electron-positron pair production from the quantum vacuum in a strong electric field (Schwinger, 1951). The analogous process for magnetic fields — and for combined electromagnetic fields — has been studied extensively in the context of magnetar physics and black hole ergospheres.

The critical field strength for spontaneous pair production is the Schwinger field:

BSch=me2c3eℏ≈4.414×1013,Gauss_BSch_​=_e_ℏ_me_2​_c_3​≈4.414×1013,Gauss

**Unicode:** B_Sch = m_e² × c³ / (e × ℏ) ≈ 4.414 × 10¹³ Gauss

**LaTeX:**

BSch=me2c3eℏ≈4.414×1013,Gauss_BSch_​=_e_ℏ_me_2​_c_3​≈4.414×1013,Gauss

**Mathematica:**

```
BSch = (me^2 * c^3) / (e * hbar)
(* Numerically: 4.414e13 Gauss *)
```

**Python (SymPy / SciPy):**

```
from scipy.constants import m_e, c, e, hbar
BSch = m_e**2 * c**3 / (e * hbar)
print(f"B_Schwinger = {BSch:.3e} T")  # ≈ 4.414e9 T
```

At field strengths below BSch_BSch_​, the pair production rate per unit volume is exponentially suppressed:

Γ≈αemB2π2exp⁡!(−πBSchB)⋅∑n=1∞(−1)n+1nexp⁡!(−nπBSchB)Γ≈_π_2_αem_​_B_2​exp!(−_BπBSch_​​)⋅_n_=1∑∞​_n_(−1)_n_+1​exp!(−_BnπBSch_​​)

where αem=e2/(ℏc)≈1/137_αem_​=_e_2/(ℏ_c_)≈1/137 is the fine structure constant.

For B≪BSch_B_≪_BSch_​, this rate is negligible. For B∼BSch_B_∼_BSch_​, the rate becomes substantial.

**LaTeX:**

Γ≈αemB2π2exp⁡!(−πBSchB)Γ≈_π_2_αem_​_B_2​exp!(−_BπBSch_​​)

(leading term of the full series)

**Mathematica:**

```
alpha = 1/137;
Gamma[B_] := (alpha * B^2 / Pi^2) * Exp[-Pi*BSch/B]
```

**Python (SymPy):**

```
from sympy import symbols, exp, pi
alpha_em, B, BSch_sym = symbols('alpha_em B B_Sch', positive=True)
Gamma = (alpha_em * B**2 / pi**2) * exp(-pi * BSch_sym / B)
```

The observed field near M87* (B∼104_B_∼104–106106 Gauss) is far below BSch_BSch_​. The Schwinger pair production rate in the M87 photon ring region is effectively zero by this formula.

However, this is not the relevant calculation. Two corrections are required.

**First:** the Schwinger formula applies to a uniform static field. In the rotating magnetosphere of a Kerr black hole, the relevant quantity is the invariant electromagnetic field strength, and the combination of electric and magnetic fields generated by the rotating ergosphere can produce effective field strengths substantially exceeding the local magnetic field alone (Blandford & Znajek, 1977; Ruffini & Wilson, 1975).

**Second:** pair production in strong gravitational fields is enhanced by gravitational analogs of the Schwinger mechanism (Gibbons, 1975; Parker, 1969). The Hawking radiation mechanism is a specific instance of this class. While Hawking radiation for stellar-mass and supermassive black holes is negligible in isolation, the interaction of Hawking-type processes with the strong electromagnetic environment of an accreting black hole is not fully characterized.

The present document does not claim that Schwinger pair production at the Schwinger rate is occurring near M87*. It claims the following, more narrowly:

> The black hole environment combines extreme magnetic fields, extreme energy densities, and extreme spacetime curvature in a regime where the standard Schwinger calculation does not straightforwardly apply. The actual pair production rate in this combined environment is not well-characterized observationally. It may be substantially higher than the vacuum Schwinger rate at the ambient field alone would suggest.

---

### **3.3 Magnetic Separation and Annihilation Suppression**

The key step in the mass generation hypothesis is not pair production per se — it is the subsequent separation of the produced pair by the magnetic field, which suppresses annihilation and allows the produced mass to persist.

A charged particle moving in a magnetic field executes circular motion (cyclotron motion) with radius:

rc=p∣q∣B=γmv∣q∣B_rc_​=∣_q_∣_Bp_​=∣_q_∣_Bγmv_​

where p=γmv_p_=_γmv_ is the relativistic momentum.

**Unicode:** r_c = p / (|q| × B) = γ × m × v / (|q| × B)

**LaTeX:**

rc=p∣q∣B=γmv∣q∣B_rc_​=∣_q_∣_Bp_​=∣_q_∣_Bγmv_​

**Mathematica:**

```
rc[p_, q_, B_] := p / (Abs[q] * B)
rc[gamma_*m_*v_, q_, B_] := gamma*m*v / (Abs[q]*B)
```

**Python (SymPy):**

```
from sympy import symbols, Abs
p, q, B, gamma, m, v = symbols('p q B gamma m v', real=True)
rc = p / (Abs(q) * B)
```

Crucially: **the sign of the charge reverses the direction of circular motion.** A positively charged particle and a negatively charged particle with the same initial velocity in the same magnetic field orbit in opposite directions and drift in opposite directions under any non-uniformity of the field (gradient drift, curvature drift).

In a uniform magnetic field with a superimposed gravitational center (the black hole), the drift velocity due to combined gravitational and magnetic forces is:

vgrav−drift=mqg×BB2**v**_grav_−_drift_​=_qm_​_B_2**g**×**B**​

Since q_q_ has opposite sign for particle and antiparticle, the drift velocities are equal in magnitude and opposite in direction. Particle and antiparticle drift apart. The spatial separation rate increases with the gravitational field strength — i.e., it is largest closest to the black hole.

**LaTeX:**

vdrift=mqg×BB2**v**_drift_​=_qm_​_B_2**g**×**B**​

**Mathematica:**

```
vDrift[m_, q_, g_, B_] := (m/q) * Cross[g, B] / B^2
(* Note: q changes sign for antiparticle *)
```

**Python (SymPy / NumPy):**

```
import numpy as np

def grav_drift(m, q, g_vec, B_vec):
    """Gravitational drift velocity for charged particle in magnetic field."""
    B_mag_sq = np.dot(B_vec, B_vec)
    return (m / q) * np.cross(g_vec, B_vec) / B_mag_sq
```

This separation is confirmed in the Yang-Mills Collider v3.2 simulation: under combined black hole gravitational field and uniform magnetic field, particle-antiparticle pairs produced at the same point separate systematically, with the antiparticle component drifting to the side opposite the accretion disk. The effect is observed to vanish when B = 0, confirming that it is the magnetic field, not the gravitational field alone, that drives the separation. With B = 0, both components are immediately accreted.

---

### **3.4 The Dynamic Equilibrium Picture**

The combined effect of pair production, magnetic separation, and the three annihilation channels (pairwise annihilation, decay, re-accretion) suggests a picture of dynamic equilibrium rather than net mass accumulation:

dMenvdt=M˙prod−M˙ann−M˙decay−M˙acc_dtdMenv_​​=_M_˙_prod_​−_M_˙_ann_​−_M_˙_decay_​−_M_˙_acc_​

where Menv_Menv_​ is the total mass in the pair-produced environment around the black hole.

In steady state, dMenv/dt=0_dMenv_​/_dt_=0 and the environment maintains a quasi-stable mass reservoir. The magnitude of this reservoir is unknown. The present document identifies it as a parameter that should be included — with appropriate uncertainty — in galactic mass models.

**LaTeX:**

dMenvdt=M˙prod−M˙ann−M˙decay−M˙acc_dtdMenv_​​=_M_˙_prod_​−_M_˙_ann_​−_M_˙_decay_​−_M_˙_acc_​

**Mathematica:**

```
Menv'[t] == Mprod[t] - Mann[t] - Mdecay[t] - Macc[t]
```

**Python (SymPy):**

```
from sympy import Function, symbols
t = symbols('t')
Menv = Function('M_env')
Mprod, Mann, Mdecay, Macc = [Function(n) for n in ['Mprod','Mann','Mdecay','Macc']]
eq = Eq(Menv(t).diff(t), Mprod(t) - Mann(t) - Mdecay(t) - Macc(t))
```

---

### **3.5 Connection to Section 1: Mass Variability and Retarded Gravity**

The dynamic equilibrium picture of Section 3.4 connects directly to the retarded gravity mechanism of Section 1 through the M¨BH_M_¨_BH_​ term in the Yahalom acceleration formula (Section 1.3.3).

If the effective gravitational mass of the black hole system — including the pair-produced environment — fluctuates on timescales shorter than the light-travel time across the galaxy, then M¨BH≠0_M_¨_BH_​=0 even for a geometrically stationary black hole. The retarded gravity term generates an acceleration even without spatial black hole displacement.

The two mechanisms are therefore additive and independent:

- Spatial displacement of the BH → retarded position offset → spiral structure
    
- Temporal fluctuation of the BH effective mass → retarded M¨_M_¨ term → additional galactic disk perturbation
    

The magnitude of the second effect depends entirely on the unknown quantity M˙prod_M_˙_prod_​. It is identified here as a theoretical possibility, not a quantified contribution.

---

## **Section 4. Theoretical Structure: How the Three Claims Connect**

---

The three claims of this document are independent in the sense that the falsification of any one does not require the falsification of the others. However, they are not unrelated. This section maps the logical connections.

```
curves (outer disk)          possible

```

### **4.1 The Independence Structure**

**Claim I (retarded gravity + BH displacement → spiral arms)** depends only on:

- Finite propagation speed of gravity ✓ (established)
    
- Black hole displacement ✓ (universal)
    

**Claim II (pair production → dynamic mass environment)** depends only on:

- Extreme field conditions near BH ✓ (observed)
    
- Schwinger mechanism ✓ (theoretically established; rate uncertain)
    
- Magnetic separation (confirmed in Yang-Mills v3.2 simulation)
    

**Claim III (gravitational signal attenuation → mass underestimation)** depends on:

- Finite propagation speed of gravity ✓
    
- Possible additional effects near the horizon (not established)
    

Claim I is the strongest. It requires the smallest set of assumptions, all of which are empirically grounded. Claims II and III are progressively more speculative. The document is organized accordingly: Claim I occupies Section 1, the longest and most detailed section. Claims II and III occupy Sections 3 and 2 respectively, with explicitly hedged language.

---

### **4.2 What Remains Unresolved**

The document is explicit about what it does not show:

1. **Quantitative fit to SPARC rotation curves.** GalaxyCS v4 has been compared informally to M33 data and shows qualitative agreement. A systematic fit to the SPARC database (175 galaxies) with optimized α and BH displacement parameters has not been performed.
    
2. **Pair production rate near real black holes.** The Yang-Mills v3.2 simulation demonstrates magnetic separation of produced pairs in a toy environment. The actual pair production rate in the combined electromagnetic-gravitational environment of a real AGN is not computed here.
    
3. **Analytical derivation of spiral pitch angle.** The simulator demonstrates spiral formation. An analytical derivation of pitch angle as a function of α, BH displacement, and disk parameters would substantially strengthen the theoretical case.
    
4. **N-body convergence.** The simulation uses 20,000–80,000 test particles. Whether the qualitative behavior is preserved at N=105_N_=105–106106 has not been verified.
    

---

### **5.1 MOND (Milgrom, 1983)**

Modified Newtonian Dynamics introduces a critical acceleration scale a0≈1.2×10−10_a_0​≈1.2×10−10 m/s²:

μ!(aa0)a=aN_μ_!(_a_0​_a_​)_a_=_aN_​

where μ(x)→1_μ_(_x_)→1 for x≫1_x_≫1 (Newtonian regime) and μ(x)→x_μ_(_x_)→_x_ for x≪1_x_≪1 (deep MOND regime).

**Unicode:** μ(a/a₀) × a = a_N

**LaTeX:**

μ!(aa0)a=aN_μ_!(_a_0​_a_​)_a_=_aN_​

**Mathematica:**

```
mu[x_] := x / Sqrt[1 + x^2]  (* one common interpolation function *)
MONDeq := mu[a/a0] * a == aN
```

**Python (SymPy):**

```
from sympy import symbols, sqrt
a, a0, aN = symbols('a a_0 a_N', positive=True)
mu = a/a0 / sqrt(1 + (a/a0)**2)
MOND_eq = Eq(mu * a, aN)
```

MOND fits individual galaxy rotation curves with remarkable accuracy given one free parameter (a0_a_0​). It fails at galaxy cluster scales without additional dark matter. It does not address spiral arm formation. The present framework requires only α_α_, makes no modification to the dynamical law itself (only to the propagation structure), and directly produces spiral structure as a byproduct.

### **5.2 Verlinde Emergent Gravity (2017)**

Verlinde’s framework derives a dark matter-like effect from the entropy associated with the de Sitter horizon:

gVerlinde=a0aB6_gVerlinde_​=6_a_0​_aB_​​​

where aB=GMb/r2_aB_​=_GMb_​/_r_2 is the baryonic gravitational acceleration and a0=cH0/6π_a_0​=_cH_0​/6_π_ (H0_H_0​: Hubble constant).

The prediction is parameter-free given known cosmological quantities. It fits the radial acceleration relation (McGaugh, Lelli & Schombert, 2016) comparably to MOND. Like MOND, it does not address spiral structure.

### **5.3 Topology-Induced Nonlocal Gravity (2025)**

Recent work on nonlocal gravity via spacetime topology introduces corrections that are non-local in the sense of depending on the global topology of the mass distribution. This is structurally distinct from the present framework, which is local (the retardation depends only on r/c_r_/_c_ for each star-BH pair) but temporally nonlocal.

## **Section 6. Simulation Infrastructure**

### **6.1 GalaxyCS v4 (Galaxy-Scale)**

**Purpose:** Demonstrate spiral arm formation via retarded gravity and BH displacement; reproduce flat rotation curves.

**Integrator:** Leapfrog (velocity Verlet), second-order, symplectic.

**Mass model:** Freeman (1970) exponential disk + central Kerr BH.

**Retarded gravity:** Explicit position history buffer (FIFO, 300 steps max). Retarded BH position recovered by linear interpolation.

**Frame-dragging:** Lense-Thirring approximation:

aLT=2GJc2r3(v×r^)**a**_LT_​=_c_2_r_32_GJ_​(**v**×_r_^)

**Unit system:** Physical units throughout.

QuantitySimulation unitSI equivalentLength1 kpc3.086×10193.086×1019 mTime1 Myr3.156×10133.156×1013 sMass1 M⊙_M_⊙​1.989×10301.989×1030 kgVelocitykpc/Myr≈0.978≈0.978 km/s

**Conserved quantities monitored:** Total energy E_E_, total angular momentum L_L_. Fractional deviations ΔE/E0Δ_E_/_E_0​ and ΔL/L0Δ_L_/_L_0​ displayed in real time.

**Lense-Thirring LaTeX:**

aLT=2GJc2r3(v×r^)**a**_LT_​=_c_2_r_32_GJ_​(**v**×_r_^)

**Mathematica:**

```
aLT[J_, r_, v_] := (2*G*J) / (c^2 * r^3) * Cross[v, rhat]
```

**Python (NumPy):**

```
def lense_thirring(J, r_vec, v_vec, G, c):
    r = np.linalg.norm(r_vec)
    r_hat = r_vec / r
    return (2*G*J) / (c**2 * r**3) * np.cross(v_vec, r_hat)
```

---

### **6.2 Yang-Mills Collider v3.2 (Particle-Scale)**

**Purpose:** Demonstrate magnetic separation of particle-antiparticle pairs in the combined gravitational and magnetic field environment near a black hole.

**Integrator:** Boris algorithm — the standard method for charged particle motion in electromagnetic fields, used in particle-in-cell (PIC) codes.

The Boris push:

t=qB2γmΔt,s=2t1+∣t∣2**t**=2_γmq_**B**​Δ_t_,**s**=1+∣**t**∣22**t**​

v′=v−+v−×t**v**′=**v**−+**v**−×**t**

v+=v−+v′×s**v**+=**v**−+**v**′×**s**

**LaTeX:**

t=qBΔt2γm,s=2t1+t2,v+=v−+(v−+v−×t)×s**t**=2_γmq_**B**Δ_t_​,**s**=1+_t_22**t**​,**v**+=**v**−+(**v**−+**v**−×**t**)×**s**

**Mathematica:**

```
t = q*B*dt/(2*gamma*m);
s = 2*t/(1 + t.t);
vPrime = vMinus + Cross[vMinus, t];
vPlus = vMinus + Cross[vPrime, s];
```

**Python (NumPy):**

```
def boris_push(v, q, m, B, E, dt, gamma):
    t = q * B * dt / (2 * gamma * m)
    s = 2 * t / (1 + np.dot(t, t))
    v_minus = v + q * E * dt / (2 * gamma * m)
    v_prime = v_minus + np.cross(v_minus, t)
    v_plus = v_minus + np.cross(v_prime, s)
    return v_plus + q * E * dt / (2 * gamma * m)
```

The Boris algorithm is time-reversible and preserves phase-space volume (symplectic). It is the gold standard for charged particle orbit integration in plasma physics and accelerator physics.

**Decay data:** PDG2022 branching ratios are used for all unstable particles produced in simulated collisions. Key channels:

- μ±→e±νˉeνμ_μ_±→_e_±_ν_ˉ_e_​_νμ_​ (BR = 100%)
    
- KS0→π+π−_KS_0​→_π_+_π_− (BR = 69.2%)
    
- B±→D0π±_B_±→_D_0_π_± (BR ≈ 0.49%)
    

**Displaced vertex tracking:** Particles with non-zero lifetime are propagated from their production vertex. The displacement between production and decay vertex:

d=cτβγ=cτpm0c_d_=_cτβγ_=_cτm_0​_cp_​

is recorded and histogrammed. This allows measurement of the effective lifetime of magnetically separated antiparticle populations.

**LaTeX:**

d=cτβγ=pτm0_d_=_cτβγ_=_m_0​_pτ_​

**Mathematica:**

```
displacement[tau_, p_, m0_] := c * tau * p / (m0 * c)
```

**Python:**

```
def displaced_vertex(tau, p, m0, c):
    return c * tau * p / (m0 * c)
```

### **A.2 Full Equation of Motion**

**Unicode:** d²r/dt² = −G × M_BH × (r − r_BH) / |r − r_BH|³ − G × M_disk(r) × r / r³ + α × G × M_BH’‘(t) / (2c²) × R_hat

**LaTeX:**

d2rdt2=−GMBH(r−rBHret)∣r−rBHret∣3−GMdisk(r)r3r+αGM¨BH2c2R^_dt_2_d_2**r**​=−∣**r**−**r**_BHret_​∣3_GMBH_​(**r**−**r**_BHret_​)​−_r_3_GMdisk_​(_r_)​**r**+2_c_2_αGM_¨_BH_​​_R_^

**Mathematica:**

```
r''[t] == -(G*MBH*(r[t] - rBHret[t]))/Norm[r[t]-rBHret[t]]^3
         - G*Mdisk[Norm[r[t]]]*r[t]/Norm[r[t]]^3
         + alpha*G*MBHdd[t]/(2*c^2) * Rhat[t]
```

**Python (conceptual):**

```
def acceleration(r, rBH_ret, MBH, MBH_dd, alpha, G, c, Mdisk_func):
    dr = r - rBH_ret
    r_mag = np.linalg.norm(r)
    dr_mag = np.linalg.norm(dr)
    R_hat = dr / dr_mag
    
    a_BH    = -G * MBH * dr / dr_mag**3
    a_disk  = -G * Mdisk_func(r_mag) * r / r_mag**3
    a_ret   =  alpha * G * MBH_dd / (2 * c**2) * R_hat
    
    return a_BH + a_disk + a_ret
```

### **A.3 Freeman (1970) Disk**

Surface density:

Σ(r)=Σ0exp⁡!(−rRd)Σ(_r_)=Σ0​exp!(−_Rd_​_r_​)

Enclosed mass:

Mdisk(r)=Mtotal[1−(1+rRd)e−r/Rd]_Mdisk_​(_r_)=_Mtotal_​[1−(1+_Rd_​_r_​)_e_−_r_/_Rd_​]

Circular velocity from disk alone (Binney & Tremaine, 2nd ed., eq. 2.165):

vc2(r)=4πGΣ0Rd⋅y2[I0(y)K0(y)−I1(y)K1(y)]_vc_2​(_r_)=4_πG_Σ0​_Rd_​⋅_y_2[_I_0​(_y_)_K_0​(_y_)−_I_1​(_y_)_K_1​(_y_)]

wherey=r/(2Rd)_y_=_r_/(2_Rd_​)andIn_In_​,Kn_Kn_​are modified Bessel functions of the first and second kind.

**LaTeX:**

vc2(r)=4πGΣ0Rdy2[I0(y)K0(y)−I1(y)K1(y)],y=r2Rd_vc_2​(_r_)=4_πG_Σ0​_Rd_​_y_2[_I_0​(_y_)_K_0​(_y_)−_I_1​(_y_)_K_1​(_y_)],_y_=2_Rd_​_r_​

**Mathematica:**

```
y[r_] := r / (2*Rd)
vc2disk[r_] := 4*Pi*G*Sigma0*Rd * y[r]^2 *
    (BesselI[0,y[r]]*BesselK[0,y[r]] - BesselI[1,y[r]]*BesselK[1,y[r]])
```

**Python (SciPy):**

```
from scipy.special import i0, i1, k0, k1
import numpy as np

def vc_disk_squared(r, G, Sigma0, Rd):
    y = r / (2*Rd)
    return 4*np.pi*G*Sigma0*Rd * y**2 * (i0(y)*k0(y) - i1(y)*k1(y))
```
